People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Yeah, um… I’m here because things kind of blew up at work. I’m in HR, and I pushed ahead with this onboarding launch before the approvals were actually in place. At the time it felt like the right move—bold, necessary, like if I didn’t keep momentum we’d lose the whole thing.

But I committed company money and vendor time, and now… I’m on leave, and everyone keeps asking what I was thinking. Which is fair, I guess. I mean, the vendor was ready, the materials were basically there, and I’ve seen launches die in committee before—sorry, that’s probably not the point. I mean… at the time I thought I had thought it through, but looking back, no, not really. I was focused on keeping the onboarding launch moving, because it felt bold and necessary, like if we slowed down it would just get buried.

I mean… not usually like this, I don’t think. I’ve made quick calls before—HR is full of situations where you have to decide something before everyone has had three meetings about it. Um… personal life? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m usually impulsive, but maybe I’m not the best judge of that right now.

Yeah… I think that’s actually a better way to describe it than just “work impulse.” It wasn’t only the onboarding thing.

I’ve been trying to run this HR redesign at the same time as lining up gallery partners—my wife keeps saying I talk about the gallery like it’s already a second job, which, maybe it kind of is—and then I was also contacting festival organizers, because I had this idea that there could be some crossover or exposure there. It felt like every idea had to be acted on immediately. Like if I didn’t move on it right then, the whole opportunity would disappear. Looking back, it was a lot of different lanes at the same time. At the time it just felt… efficient, I guess. Or necessary.

Manipulative? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not that I can remember someone saying it that directly, anyway.

I mean, people use that word in different ways, right? Like in HR, someone might say, “That person is being manipulative,” when really they mean the person is just advocating for themselves badly, or they’re emotional, or they’re trying to get a meeting moved. So I’m probably overly sensitive to the word because I hear it thrown around at work.

Personally, people close to me might say I can be argumentative or too persistent when I think I’m right. But manipulative—like scheming or trying to control people—I don’t think I’ve been described that way. Not by someone who knows me well, at least.

Yeah, probably. I mean, I think most people have had that happen, right? Someone says, “You’re not listening to me,” or “That came out harsher than you meant,” and then you realize halfway through explaining yourself that explaining is maybe not helping.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.

I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.

The point is I made decisions I shouldn’t have made, and now I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you thought through all the potential consequences before you decided to move ahead with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: I mean… at the time I thought I had thought it through, but looking back, no, not really. I was focused on keeping the onboarding launch moving, because it felt bold and necessary, like if we slowed down it would just get buried.\n\nAnd I know approvals matter—I work in HR, I’m not new to that. But I still committed company money and vendor time before everything was actually signed off. I kept thinking about how many good projects die because everyone wants one more meeting, one more revision—my last company was like that, we had this whole training rollout that sat for months because nobody wanted to be the person to say yes.\n\nBut… yeah, that’s not an excuse. Now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and honestly I don’t have a great answer. At the time it felt necessary. I’ve made quick calls before—HR is full of situations where you have to decide something before everyone has had three meetings about it. At my last job, we had this onboarding packet that took forever because legal kept changing one paragraph, and by the end nobody even cared anymore.\n\nBut this… this was bigger. I pushed ahead with the launch and committed company money and vendor time before the approvals were actually in place. At the time it felt bold, like the necessary thing to do to keep everything moving. Now I’m on leave, and everyone’s asking what I was thinking, so… yeah, I guess this one crossed a line.\n\nDoctor: Have you often acted on impulse in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um… personal life? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m usually impulsive, but maybe I’m not the best judge of that right now.\n\nLike, I’ll make quick decisions sometimes—buying something for the apartment because I’m convinced it’ll make everything more organized, or deciding at 8 p.m. that I need to rearrange a whole room. But that feels different from what happened at work. Or maybe it doesn’t, I don’t know.\n\nMy wife would probably have a more honest answer. I tend to think of myself as decisive, not impulsive, but… lately I’m not sure where that line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were consistently acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I keep coming back to this onboarding launch because that’s the thing everyone can point to now. I pushed it ahead before the approvals were actually in place, committed company money and vendor time, and at the time it felt like I was just being decisive—like, bold, necessary, keeping the whole thing from getting stuck.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but HR is always talking about process and guardrails, and there I was basically stepping around them. And I remember thinking, “If we wait, this will die in committee,” because I’ve seen that happen before with projects that just sit there until no one has energy for them anymore.\n\nBut now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and… yeah. I guess that was a period where I was acting on impulse more than I realized. At the time it didn’t feel impulsive. It felt urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt like you were acting on impulse in multiple areas of your life at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a better way to describe it than just “work impulse.” It wasn’t only the onboarding thing.\n\nI’ve been trying to run this HR redesign at the same time as lining up gallery partners—my wife keeps saying I talk about the gallery like it’s already a second job, which, maybe it kind of is—and then I was also contacting festival organizers, because I had this idea that there could be some crossover or exposure there. My brain went to my sister’s situation because she’s always dealing with school forms and it drives her crazy.\n\nBut for me, outside work, I can’t think of some big pattern of ignoring rules or disrespecting authority. I might argue if something seems inefficient or arbitrary, but that’s more… I don’t know, being opinionated. I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.

I tend to think of myself as decisive, not impulsive, but… lately I’m not sure where that line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were consistently acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I keep coming back to this onboarding launch because that’s the thing everyone can point to now. I pushed it ahead before the approvals were actually in place, committed company money and vendor time, and at the time it felt like I was just being decisive—like, bold, necessary, keeping the whole thing from getting stuck.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but HR is always talking about process and guardrails, and there I was basically stepping around them. And I remember thinking, “If we wait, this will die in committee,” because I’ve seen that happen before with projects that just sit there until no one has energy for them anymore.\n\nBut now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and… yeah. I guess that was a period where I was acting on impulse more than I realized. At the time it didn’t feel impulsive. It felt urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt like you were acting on impulse in multiple areas of your life at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a better way to describe it than just “work impulse.” It wasn’t only the onboarding thing.\n\nI’ve been trying to run this HR redesign at the same time as lining up gallery partners—my wife keeps saying I talk about the gallery like it’s already a second job, which, maybe it kind of is—and then I was also contacting festival organizers, because I had this idea that there could be some crossover or exposure there. And on top of that I was trying to fix several staff conflicts at once, like if I could just get everyone in the right room or send the right email, it would all resolve.\n\nIt felt like every idea had to be acted on immediately. Like if I didn’t move on it right then, the whole opportunity would disappear. Looking back, it was a lot of different lanes at the same time. At the time it just felt… efficient, I guess. Or necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble following rules or respecting authority outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a problem with authority, exactly.\n\nI mean, rules… it depends what kind of rules. Like parking signs, HOA stuff, school forms for the kids—well, not kids, I don’t know why I said that, we don’t—sorry. My brain went to my sister’s situation because she’s always dealing with school forms and it drives her crazy.\n\nBut for me, outside work, I can’t think of some big pattern of ignoring rules or disrespecting authority. I might argue if something seems inefficient or arbitrary, but that’s more… I don’t know, being opinionated. I’m not out there trying to break rules just to break them.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for breaking rules or disrupting others when you were a child, before you turned 15?\n\nPatient: Before 15… I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I got told to stop talking sometimes or got in trouble for being sarcastic, but I don’t remember some big pattern of breaking rules or disrupting everyone.\n\nMy parents might tell it differently—parents always have those stories, like “you were impossible at dinner” or whatever—but nothing stands out to me like suspensions or constantly being in trouble. I was more… opinionated, maybe. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been arrested or in legal trouble as an adult?\n\nPatient: Legal trouble? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had boring adult stuff—parking tickets, maybe a late fee that made me irrationally annoyed because the website didn’t process something right, and then you end up on hold listening to that terrible music for 40 minutes.\n\nBut arrested? No. Nothing like that. I haven’t had court cases or anything serious as an adult that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described as manipulative by someone you know well?\n\nPatient: Manipulative? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not that I can remember someone saying it that directly, anyway.\n\nI mean, people use that word in different ways, right? Like in HR, someone might say, “That person is being manipulative,” when really they mean the person is just advocating for themselves badly, or they’re emotional, or they’re trying to get a meeting moved. So I’m probably overly sensitive to the word because I hear it thrown around at work.\n\nPersonally, people close to me might say I can be argumentative or too persistent when I think I’m right. But manipulative—like scheming or trying to control people—I don’t think I’ve been described that way. Not by someone who knows me well, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you hurt someone's feelings or didn't consider their perspective in a situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. Someone says, “You’re not listening to me,” or “That came out harsher than you meant,” and then you realize halfway through explaining yourself that explaining is maybe not helping.\n\nI can be pretty focused on the point I’m trying to make, especially if I think something is unfair or inefficient. In HR that’s tricky because you’re supposed to be the person who considers everyone’s perspective, and sometimes people assume you’re taking a side when you’re really just trying to follow the policy. Policy language is terrible, by the way—like, nobody talks that way in real life.\n\nBut yes, I’ve been told I was being insensitive or not seeing where someone else was coming from. I don’t think I usually mean to hurt anyone, but I can miss it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you seem to lack empathy or don't care about how others feel?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.\n\nSo maybe someone has felt that way about me, but I don’t remember being told, “You don’t care how people feel.” I do care. I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that you knew would hurt someone else, without feeling bad about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.\n\nThe “without feeling bad” part is the tricky piece. I can get very… convinced that I’m right in the moment, and then later I’ll replay it and think, okay, maybe I was too sharp there. But doing something specifically because I knew it would hurt someone and just not caring? I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.

So no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.

I don’t think I usually mean to hurt anyone, but I can miss it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you seem to lack empathy or don't care about how others feel?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.\n\nSo maybe someone has felt that way about me, but I don’t remember being told, “You don’t care how people feel.” I do care. I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that you knew would hurt someone else, without feeling bad about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.\n\nThe “without feeling bad” part is the tricky piece. I can get very… convinced that I’m right in the moment, and then later I’ll replay it and think, okay, maybe I was too sharp there. But doing something specifically because I knew it would hurt someone and just not caring? I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that disregarded someone else's rights or feelings without feeling remorseful about it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.\n\nBut, um… feelings? I’ve probably brushed past someone’s feelings in the moment. Like if I’m trying to solve the problem, I can get a little too locked in on, “Okay, what’s the process, what’s the next step,” and miss that the person is upset. But afterwards, if I realize I was dismissive, I usually do feel bad about it. I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, relationships end for different reasons, right? Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Or you’re just exhausted.

I don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.

So… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.

Which is fair, I guess. I mean, the vendor was ready, the materials were basically there, and I’ve seen launches die in committee before—sorry, that’s probably not the point. The point is I made decisions I shouldn’t have made, and now I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you thought through all the potential consequences before you decided to move ahead with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: I mean… at the time I thought I had thought it through, but looking back, no, not really. I was focused on keeping the onboarding launch moving, because it felt bold and necessary, like if we slowed down it would just get buried.\n\nAnd I know approvals matter—I work in HR, I’m not new to that. But I still committed company money and vendor time before everything was actually signed off. I kept thinking about how many good projects die because everyone wants one more meeting, one more revision—my last company was like that, we had this whole training rollout that sat for months because nobody wanted to be the person to say yes.\n\nBut… yeah, that’s not an excuse. Now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and honestly I don’t have a great answer. At the time it felt necessary. Now it feels like I skipped over consequences I should’ve taken seriously.\n\nDoctor: Did you often act on impulse like this before the incident with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: I mean… not usually like this, I don’t think. I’ve made quick calls before—HR is full of situations where you have to decide something before everyone has had three meetings about it. At my last job, we had this onboarding packet that took forever because legal kept changing one paragraph, and by the end nobody even cared anymore.\n\nBut this… this was bigger. I pushed ahead with the launch and committed company money and vendor time before the approvals were actually in place. At the time it felt bold, like the necessary thing to do to keep everything moving. Now I’m on leave, and everyone’s asking what I was thinking, so… yeah, I guess this one crossed a line.\n\nDoctor: Have you often acted on impulse in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um… personal life? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m usually impulsive, but maybe I’m not the best judge of that right now.\n\nLike, I’ll make quick decisions sometimes—buying something for the apartment because I’m convinced it’ll make everything more organized, or deciding at 8 p.m. that I need to rearrange a whole room. But that feels different from what happened at work. Or maybe it doesn’t, I don’t know.\n\nMy wife would probably have a more honest answer. I tend to think of myself as decisive, not impulsive, but… lately I’m not sure where that line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were consistently acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I keep coming back to this onboarding launch because that’s the thing everyone can point to now. I pushed it ahead before the approvals were actually in place, committed company money and vendor time, and at the time it felt like I was just being decisive—like, bold, necessary, keeping the whole thing from getting stuck.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but HR is always talking about process and guardrails, and there I was basically stepping around them. And I remember thinking, “If we wait, this will die in committee,” because I’ve seen that happen before with projects that just sit there until no one has energy for them anymore.\n\nBut now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and… yeah. I guess that was a period where I was acting on impulse more than I realized. At the time it didn’t feel impulsive. It felt urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt like you were acting on impulse in multiple areas of your life at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a better way to describe it than just “work impulse.” It wasn’t only the onboarding thing.\n\nI’ve been trying to run this HR redesign at the same time as lining up gallery partners—my wife keeps saying I talk about the gallery like it’s already a second job, which, maybe it kind of is—and then I was also contacting festival organizers, because I had this idea that there could be some crossover or exposure there. And on top of that I was trying to fix several staff conflicts at once, like if I could just get everyone in the right room or send the right email, it would all resolve.\n\nIt felt like every idea had to be acted on immediately. Like if I didn’t move on it right then, the whole opportunity would disappear. Looking back, it was a lot of different lanes at the same time. At the time it just felt… efficient, I guess. Or necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble following rules or respecting authority outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a problem with authority, exactly.\n\nI mean, rules… it depends what kind of rules. Like parking signs, HOA stuff, school forms for the kids—well, not kids, I don’t know why I said that, we don’t—sorry. My brain went to my sister’s situation because she’s always dealing with school forms and it drives her crazy.\n\nBut for me, outside work, I can’t think of some big pattern of ignoring rules or disrespecting authority. I might argue if something seems inefficient or arbitrary, but that’s more… I don’t know, being opinionated. I’m not out there trying to break rules just to break them.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for breaking rules or disrupting others when you were a child, before you turned 15?\n\nPatient: Before 15… I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I got told to stop talking sometimes or got in trouble for being sarcastic, but I don’t remember some big pattern of breaking rules or disrupting everyone.\n\nMy parents might tell it differently—parents always have those stories, like “you were impossible at dinner” or whatever—but nothing stands out to me like suspensions or constantly being in trouble. I was more… opinionated, maybe. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been arrested or in legal trouble as an adult?\n\nPatient: Legal trouble? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had boring adult stuff—parking tickets, maybe a late fee that made me irrationally annoyed because the website didn’t process something right, and then you end up on hold listening to that terrible music for 40 minutes.\n\nBut arrested? No. Nothing like that. I haven’t had court cases or anything serious as an adult that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described as manipulative by someone you know well?\n\nPatient: Manipulative? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not that I can remember someone saying it that directly, anyway.\n\nI mean, people use that word in different ways, right? Like in HR, someone might say, “That person is being manipulative,” when really they mean the person is just advocating for themselves badly, or they’re emotional, or they’re trying to get a meeting moved. So I’m probably overly sensitive to the word because I hear it thrown around at work.\n\nPersonally, people close to me might say I can be argumentative or too persistent when I think I’m right. But manipulative—like scheming or trying to control people—I don’t think I’ve been described that way. Not by someone who knows me well, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you hurt someone's feelings or didn't consider their perspective in a situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, I think most people have had that happen, right? Someone says, “You’re not listening to me,” or “That came out harsher than you meant,” and then you realize halfway through explaining yourself that explaining is maybe not helping.\n\nI can be pretty focused on the point I’m trying to make, especially if I think something is unfair or inefficient. In HR that’s tricky because you’re supposed to be the person who considers everyone’s perspective, and sometimes people assume you’re taking a side when you’re really just trying to follow the policy. Policy language is terrible, by the way—like, nobody talks that way in real life.\n\nBut yes, I’ve been told I was being insensitive or not seeing where someone else was coming from. I don’t think I usually mean to hurt anyone, but I can miss it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you seem to lack empathy or don't care about how others feel?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.\n\nSo maybe someone has felt that way about me, but I don’t remember being told, “You don’t care how people feel.” I do care. I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that you knew would hurt someone else, without feeling bad about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.\n\nThe “without feeling bad” part is the tricky piece. I can get very… convinced that I’m right in the moment, and then later I’ll replay it and think, okay, maybe I was too sharp there. But doing something specifically because I knew it would hurt someone and just not caring? I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that disregarded someone else's rights or feelings without feeling remorseful about it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.\n\nBut, um… feelings? I’ve probably brushed past someone’s feelings in the moment. Like if I’m trying to solve the problem, I can get a little too locked in on, “Okay, what’s the process, what’s the next step,” and miss that the person is upset. But afterwards, if I realize I was dismissive, I usually do feel bad about it. I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever ended a relationship without feeling much emotional distress about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, relationships end for different reasons, right? Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Or you’re just exhausted.\n\nI don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F60.0", "F60.2", "F60.3"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever ended a relationship without feeling much emotional distress about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, relationships end for different reasons, right? Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Or you’re just exhausted.\n\nI don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever deliberately deceived or lied to someone for personal gain?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 16\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is about deliberate deceit or lying for personal gain, which is not covered by the provided Bipolar I symptom profile items."}

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, “personal gain” makes me think of, like, fraud or manipulating someone to get money or a promotion or something, and no, I don’t have anything like that.

Have I ever softened the truth? Sure. Like saying, “I’m almost there,” when I’m absolutely not almost there, which everyone does and somehow we all agree to keep pretending it means something. Or at work, phrasing something more carefully because if you say it too directly people stop hearing the point and start reacting to the wording.

But deliberately lying to get something for myself… I’m not saying I’ve never told a lie in my life, but I can’t think of an example like that. If anything, I get too caught up in explaining myself, which is probably why a simple question turns into all this.

I don’t know. But that feels different from what happened at work. Or maybe it doesn’t, I don’t know.\n\nMy wife would probably have a more honest answer. I tend to think of myself as decisive, not impulsive, but… lately I’m not sure where that line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were consistently acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Or necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble following rules or respecting authority outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a problem with authority, exactly.\n\nI mean, rules… it depends what kind of rules. Like parking signs, HOA stuff, school forms for the kids—well, not kids, I don’t know why I said that, we don’t—sorry. My brain went to my sister’s situation because she’s always dealing with school forms and it drives her crazy.\n\nBut for me, outside work, I can’t think of some big pattern of ignoring rules or disrespecting authority. I might argue if something seems inefficient or arbitrary, but that’s more… I don’t know, being opinionated. I’m not out there trying to break rules just to break them.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for breaking rules or disrupting others when you were a child, before you turned 15?\n\nPatient: Before 15… I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I got told to stop talking sometimes or got in trouble for being sarcastic, but I don’t remember some big pattern of breaking rules or disrupting everyone.\n\nMy parents might tell it differently—parents always have those stories, like “you were impossible at dinner” or whatever—but nothing stands out to me like suspensions or constantly being in trouble. I was more… opinionated, maybe. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been arrested or in legal trouble as an adult?\n\nPatient: Legal trouble? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had boring adult stuff—parking tickets, maybe a late fee that made me irrationally annoyed because the website didn’t process something right, and then you end up on hold listening to that terrible music for 40 minutes.\n\nBut arrested? No. Nothing like that. I haven’t had court cases or anything serious as an adult that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described as manipulative by someone you know well?\n\nPatient: Manipulative? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not that I can remember someone saying it that directly, anyway.\n\nI mean, people use that word in different ways, right? Like in HR, someone might say, “That person is being manipulative,” when really they mean the person is just advocating for themselves badly, or they’re emotional, or they’re trying to get a meeting moved. But manipulative—like scheming or trying to control people—I don’t think I’ve been described that way. I mean, I think most people have had that happen, right? Someone says, “You’re not listening to me,” or “That came out harsher than you meant,” and then you realize halfway through explaining yourself that explaining is maybe not helping.\n\nI can be pretty focused on the point I’m trying to make, especially if I think something is unfair or inefficient. In HR that’s tricky because you’re supposed to be the person who considers everyone’s perspective, and sometimes people assume you’re taking a side when you’re really just trying to follow the policy. Policy language is terrible, by the way—like, nobody talks that way in real life.\n\nBut yes, I’ve been told I was being insensitive or not seeing where someone else was coming from. No. Nothing like that. I haven’t had court cases or anything serious as an adult that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described as manipulative by someone you know well?\n\nPatient: Manipulative? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not that I can remember someone saying it that directly, anyway.\n\nI mean, people use that word in different ways, right? Like in HR, someone might say, “That person is being manipulative,” when really they mean the person is just advocating for themselves badly, or they’re emotional, or they’re trying to get a meeting moved. So I’m probably overly sensitive to the word because I hear it thrown around at work.\n\nPersonally, people close to me might say I can be argumentative or too persistent when I think I’m right. But manipulative—like scheming or trying to control people—I don’t think I’ve been described that way. Not by someone who knows me well, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you hurt someone's feelings or didn't consider their perspective in a situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, I think most people have had that happen, right? Someone says, “You’re not listening to me,” or “That came out harsher than you meant,” and then you realize halfway through explaining yourself that explaining is maybe not helping.\n\nI can be pretty focused on the point I’m trying to make, especially if I think something is unfair or inefficient. In HR that’s tricky because you’re supposed to be the person who considers everyone’s perspective, and sometimes people assume you’re taking a side when you’re really just trying to follow the policy. Policy language is terrible, by the way—like, nobody talks that way in real life.\n\nBut yes, I’ve been told I was being insensitive or not seeing where someone else was coming from. I don’t think I usually mean to hurt anyone, but I can miss it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you seem to lack empathy or don't care about how others feel?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.\n\nSo maybe someone has felt that way about me, but I don’t remember being told, “You don’t care how people feel.” I do care. I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that you knew would hurt someone else, without feeling bad about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.\n\nThe “without feeling bad” part is the tricky piece. I can get very… convinced that I’m right in the moment, and then later I’ll replay it and think, okay, maybe I was too sharp there. But doing something specifically because I knew it would hurt someone and just not caring? I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that disregarded someone else's rights or feelings without feeling remorseful about it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.\n\nBut, um… feelings? I’ve probably brushed past someone’s feelings in the moment. Like if I’m trying to solve the problem, I can get a little too locked in on, “Okay, what’s the process, what’s the next step,” and miss that the person is upset. But afterwards, if I realize I was dismissive, I usually do feel bad about it. I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever ended a relationship without feeling much emotional distress about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Or you’re just exhausted.\n\nI don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever deliberately deceived or lied to someone for personal gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, “personal gain” makes me think of, like, fraud or manipulating someone to get money or a promotion or something, and no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nHave I ever softened the truth? Sure. Like saying, “I’m almost there,” when I’m absolutely not almost there, which everyone does and somehow we all agree to keep pretending it means something. Or at work, phrasing something more carefully because if you say it too directly people stop hearing the point and start reacting to the wording.\n\nBut deliberately lying to get something for myself… I’m not saying I’ve never told a lie in my life, but I can’t think of an example like that. Indifferent? I don’t think so, not generally. I mean, there are times where I’m… overwhelmed, or I don’t know what to do with someone else’s pain, so I might get practical instead of warm. Like, “Okay, who do we call, what form needs to be filled out,” which is probably very comforting if what you wanted was a spreadsheet, less so if you wanted a hug.

But I wouldn’t say I don’t care. If anything, I can get stuck thinking about it later, like whether I said the right thing or made it worse. I guess with strangers on the news or something, sometimes you have to shut part of it off because there’s just too much. But that feels different from not caring about an actual person in front of me.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed myself being indifferent to someone’s suffering or well-being. More like awkward or too task-focused sometimes.

I don’t think I usually mean to hurt anyone, but I can miss it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you seem to lack empathy or don't care about how others feel?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.\n\nSo maybe someone has felt that way about me, but I don’t remember being told, “You don’t care how people feel.” I do care. I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that you knew would hurt someone else, without feeling bad about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.\n\nThe “without feeling bad” part is the tricky piece. I can get very… convinced that I’m right in the moment, and then later I’ll replay it and think, okay, maybe I was too sharp there. But doing something specifically because I knew it would hurt someone and just not caring? I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that disregarded someone else's rights or feelings without feeling remorseful about it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.\n\nBut, um… feelings? I’ve probably brushed past someone’s feelings in the moment. Like if I’m trying to solve the problem, I can get a little too locked in on, “Okay, what’s the process, what’s the next step,” and miss that the person is upset. But afterwards, if I realize I was dismissive, I usually do feel bad about it. I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever ended a relationship without feeling much emotional distress about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, relationships end for different reasons, right? Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Or you’re just exhausted.\n\nI don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever deliberately deceived or lied to someone for personal gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, “personal gain” makes me think of, like, fraud or manipulating someone to get money or a promotion or something, and no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nHave I ever softened the truth? Sure. Like saying, “I’m almost there,” when I’m absolutely not almost there, which everyone does and somehow we all agree to keep pretending it means something. Or at work, phrasing something more carefully because if you say it too directly people stop hearing the point and start reacting to the wording.\n\nBut deliberately lying to get something for myself… I’m not saying I’ve never told a lie in my life, but I can’t think of an example like that. If anything, I get too caught up in explaining myself, which is probably why a simple question turns into all this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt indifferent to someone else's suffering or well-being?\n\nPatient: Indifferent? I don’t think so, not generally. I mean, there are times where I’m… overwhelmed, or I don’t know what to do with someone else’s pain, so I might get practical instead of warm. Like, “Okay, who do we call, what form needs to be filled out,” which is probably very comforting if what you wanted was a spreadsheet, less so if you wanted a hug.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I don’t care. If anything, I can get stuck thinking about it later, like whether I said the right thing or made it worse. If anything, I get too caught up in explaining myself, which is probably why a simple question turns into all this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt indifferent to someone else's suffering or well-being?\n\nPatient: Indifferent? I don’t think so, not generally. I mean, there are times where I’m… overwhelmed, or I don’t know what to do with someone else’s pain, so I might get practical instead of warm. Like, “Okay, who do we call, what form needs to be filled out,” which is probably very comforting if what you wanted was a spreadsheet, less so if you wanted a hug.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I don’t care. If anything, I can get stuck thinking about it later, like whether I said the right thing or made it worse. I guess with strangers on the news or something, sometimes you have to shut part of it off because there’s just too much. But that feels different from not caring about an actual person in front of me.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed myself being indifferent to someone’s suffering or well-being. More like awkward or too task-focused sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have any significant behavioral problems or get into trouble at school or with the law before you were 15 years old?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 44 / human_resources_worker / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before 15? I don’t think I had anything major like that. I mean, I got in trouble in the normal school way—talking when I shouldn’t, forgetting homework, probably being sarcastic at exactly the wrong time. Teachers really love that, as you can imagine.

But significant behavioral problems… I don’t think so. No law trouble or anything like that. I was more of a “please don’t call attention to me” kid, though then again everyone remembers childhood differently, right? Like my version is all cafeteria smells and trying not to wear the wrong shoes on gym day.

So, no, nothing serious comes to mind before 15.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F60.2, F60.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because things kind of blew up at work. I’m in HR, and I pushed ahead with this onboarding launch before the approvals were actually in place. At the time it felt like the right move—bold, necessary, like if I didn’t keep momentum we’d lose the whole thing.\n\nBut I committed company money and vendor time, and now… I’m on leave, and everyone keeps asking what I was thinking. Which is fair, I guess. I mean, the vendor was ready, the materials were basically there, and I’ve seen launches die in committee before—sorry, that’s probably not the point. The point is I made decisions I shouldn’t have made, and now I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you thought through all the potential consequences before you decided to move ahead with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: I mean… at the time I thought I had thought it through, but looking back, no, not really. I was focused on keeping the onboarding launch moving, because it felt bold and necessary, like if we slowed down it would just get buried.\n\nAnd I know approvals matter—I work in HR, I’m not new to that. But I still committed company money and vendor time before everything was actually signed off. I kept thinking about how many good projects die because everyone wants one more meeting, one more revision—my last company was like that, we had this whole training rollout that sat for months because nobody wanted to be the person to say yes.\n\nBut… yeah, that’s not an excuse. Now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and honestly I don’t have a great answer. At the time it felt necessary. Now it feels like I skipped over consequences I should’ve taken seriously.\n\nDoctor: Did you often act on impulse like this before the incident with the onboarding launch?\n\nPatient: I mean… not usually like this, I don’t think. At my last job, we had this onboarding packet that took forever because legal kept changing one paragraph, and by the end nobody even cared anymore.\n\nBut this… this was bigger. I pushed ahead with the launch and committed company money and vendor time before the approvals were actually in place. At the time it felt bold, like the necessary thing to do to keep everything moving. Now I’m on leave, and everyone’s asking what I was thinking, so… yeah, I guess this one crossed a line.\n\nDoctor: Have you often acted on impulse in your personal life, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um… personal life? I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’m usually impulsive, but maybe I’m not the best judge of that right now.\n\nLike, I’ll make quick decisions sometimes—buying something for the apartment because I’m convinced it’ll make everything more organized, or deciding at 8 p.m. that I need to rearrange a whole room. But that feels different from what happened at work. Or maybe it doesn’t, I don’t know.\n\nMy wife would probably have a more honest answer. I tend to think of myself as decisive, not impulsive, but… lately I’m not sure where that line is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods in your life where you felt like you were consistently acting on impulse more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I keep coming back to this onboarding launch because that’s the thing everyone can point to now. I pushed it ahead before the approvals were actually in place, committed company money and vendor time, and at the time it felt like I was just being decisive—like, bold, necessary, keeping the whole thing from getting stuck.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but HR is always talking about process and guardrails, and there I was basically stepping around them. And I remember thinking, “If we wait, this will die in committee,” because I’ve seen that happen before with projects that just sit there until no one has energy for them anymore.\n\nBut now I’m on leave, and people are asking what I was thinking, and… yeah. I guess that was a period where I was acting on impulse more than I realized. At the time it didn’t feel impulsive. It felt urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt like you were acting on impulse in multiple areas of your life at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a better way to describe it than just “work impulse.” It wasn’t only the onboarding thing.\n\nI’ve been trying to run this HR redesign at the same time as lining up gallery partners—my wife keeps saying I talk about the gallery like it’s already a second job, which, maybe it kind of is—and then I was also contacting festival organizers, because I had this idea that there could be some crossover or exposure there. And on top of that I was trying to fix several staff conflicts at once, like if I could just get everyone in the right room or send the right email, it would all resolve.\n\nIt felt like every idea had to be acted on immediately. Like if I didn’t move on it right then, the whole opportunity would disappear. Looking back, it was a lot of different lanes at the same time. At the time it just felt… efficient, I guess. Or necessary.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble following rules or respecting authority outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think of myself as someone who has a problem with authority, exactly.\n\nI mean, rules… it depends what kind of rules. Like parking signs, HOA stuff, school forms for the kids—well, not kids, I don’t know why I said that, we don’t—sorry. My brain went to my sister’s situation because she’s always dealing with school forms and it drives her crazy.\n\nBut for me, outside work, I can’t think of some big pattern of ignoring rules or disrespecting authority. I might argue if something seems inefficient or arbitrary, but that’s more… I don’t know, being opinionated. I’m not out there trying to break rules just to break them.\n\nDoctor: Did you often get in trouble for breaking rules or disrupting others when you were a child, before you turned 15?\n\nPatient: Before 15… I don’t really know. I mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I got told to stop talking sometimes or got in trouble for being sarcastic, but I don’t remember some big pattern of breaking rules or disrupting everyone.\n\nMy parents might tell it differently—parents always have those stories, like “you were impossible at dinner” or whatever—but nothing stands out to me like suspensions or constantly being in trouble. I was more… opinionated, maybe. But I’m not sure that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been arrested or in legal trouble as an adult?\n\nPatient: Legal trouble? No, not really. I mean, I’ve had boring adult stuff—parking tickets, maybe a late fee that made me irrationally annoyed because the website didn’t process something right, and then you end up on hold listening to that terrible music for 40 minutes.\n\nBut arrested? No. Nothing like that. I haven’t had court cases or anything serious as an adult that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been described as manipulative by someone you know well?\n\nPatient: Manipulative? I don’t… I don’t think so. Not that I can remember someone saying it that directly, anyway.\n\nI mean, people use that word in different ways, right? Like in HR, someone might say, “That person is being manipulative,” when really they mean the person is just advocating for themselves badly, or they’re emotional, or they’re trying to get a meeting moved. So I’m probably overly sensitive to the word because I hear it thrown around at work.\n\nPersonally, people close to me might say I can be argumentative or too persistent when I think I’m right. But manipulative—like scheming or trying to control people—I don’t think I’ve been described that way. Not by someone who knows me well, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you hurt someone's feelings or didn't consider their perspective in a situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, I think most people have had that happen, right? Someone says, “You’re not listening to me,” or “That came out harsher than you meant,” and then you realize halfway through explaining yourself that explaining is maybe not helping.\n\nI can be pretty focused on the point I’m trying to make, especially if I think something is unfair or inefficient. In HR that’s tricky because you’re supposed to be the person who considers everyone’s perspective, and sometimes people assume you’re taking a side when you’re really just trying to follow the policy. Policy language is terrible, by the way—like, nobody talks that way in real life.\n\nBut yes, I’ve been told I was being insensitive or not seeing where someone else was coming from. I don’t think I usually mean to hurt anyone, but I can miss it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you seem to lack empathy or don't care about how others feel?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone’s put it that bluntly, like, “You lack empathy.” That sounds… pretty harsh. I’ve definitely had people tell me I came across cold or too procedural, especially at work, because HR is this weird space where you’re trying to be fair and also not accidentally promise something you can’t deliver.\n\nSo maybe someone has felt that way about me, but I don’t remember being told, “You don’t care how people feel.” I do care. I just sometimes get focused on the rule or the next step, and then later I realize the person needed me to acknowledge the feeling first, not explain the policy handbook like it’s a sacred text.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that you knew would hurt someone else, without feeling bad about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of something like that. I mean, I’ve definitely said things knowing they might land badly—like giving someone direct feedback or telling someone no when they really wanted a different answer—but that’s not the same as wanting to hurt them, I don’t think.\n\nThe “without feeling bad” part is the tricky piece. I can get very… convinced that I’m right in the moment, and then later I’ll replay it and think, okay, maybe I was too sharp there. But doing something specifically because I knew it would hurt someone and just not caring? I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that disregarded someone else's rights or feelings without feeling remorseful about it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.\n\nBut, um… feelings? I’ve probably brushed past someone’s feelings in the moment. Like if I’m trying to solve the problem, I can get a little too locked in on, “Okay, what’s the process, what’s the next step,” and miss that the person is upset. But afterwards, if I realize I was dismissive, I usually do feel bad about it. I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever ended a relationship without feeling much emotional distress about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, relationships end for different reasons, right? Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Or you’re just exhausted.\n\nI don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever deliberately deceived or lied to someone for personal gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, “personal gain” makes me think of, like, fraud or manipulating someone to get money or a promotion or something, and no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nHave I ever softened the truth? Sure. Like saying, “I’m almost there,” when I’m absolutely not almost there, which everyone does and somehow we all agree to keep pretending it means something. Or at work, phrasing something more carefully because if you say it too directly people stop hearing the point and start reacting to the wording.\n\nBut deliberately lying to get something for myself… I’m not saying I’ve never told a lie in my life, but I can’t think of an example like that. If anything, I get too caught up in explaining myself, which is probably why a simple question turns into all this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt indifferent to someone else's suffering or well-being?\n\nPatient: Indifferent? I don’t think so, not generally. I mean, there are times where I’m… overwhelmed, or I don’t know what to do with someone else’s pain, so I might get practical instead of warm. Like, “Okay, who do we call, what form needs to be filled out,” which is probably very comforting if what you wanted was a spreadsheet, less so if you wanted a hug.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I don’t care. If anything, I can get stuck thinking about it later, like whether I said the right thing or made it worse. I guess with strangers on the news or something, sometimes you have to shut part of it off because there’s just too much. But that feels different from not caring about an actual person in front of me.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed myself being indifferent to someone’s suffering or well-being. More like awkward or too task-focused sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any significant behavioral problems or get into trouble at school or with the law before you were 15 years old?\n\nPatient: Before 15? I don’t think I had anything major like that. I mean, I got in trouble in the normal school way—talking when I shouldn’t, forgetting homework, probably being sarcastic at exactly the wrong time. Teachers really love that, as you can imagine.\n\nBut significant behavioral problems… I don’t think so. No law trouble or anything like that. I was more of a “please don’t call attention to me” kid, though then again everyone remembers childhood differently, right? Like my version is all cafeteria smells and trying not to wear the wrong shoes on gym day.\n\nSo, no, nothing serious comes to mind before 15.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nThere are probably people who’d disagree with my version, I guess. People remember conversations differently. But no, not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something that disregarded someone else's rights or feelings without feeling remorseful about it afterwards?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, “disregarded someone’s rights” sounds like a legal training video, and I’ve sat through enough of those to immediately picture bad PowerPoint slides and that one narrator voice.\n\nBut, um… feelings? I’ve probably brushed past someone’s feelings in the moment. Like if I’m trying to solve the problem, I can get a little too locked in on, “Okay, what’s the process, what’s the next step,” and miss that the person is upset. But afterwards, if I realize I was dismissive, I usually do feel bad about it. I’ll replay it, which is annoying, because then I’m in the shower three days later thinking of the sentence I should’ve said.\n\nSo no, I can’t really think of something where I ignored someone’s rights or feelings and then felt no remorse. That doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever ended a relationship without feeling much emotional distress about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, relationships end for different reasons, right? Sometimes by the time it’s actually over, you’ve already done all the emotional processing in little miserable installments for months, so when the conversation finally happens you look calmer than you feel. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Or you’re just exhausted.\n\nI don’t think I’ve ended something and felt nothing, if that’s what you mean. But there have been times where I probably looked pretty composed, maybe even detached, because I was focused on getting through the logistics—whose stuff is where, what do we say to mutual friends, that kind of thing. Which is funny, not funny ha-ha, but HR brain does that too: turn a painful thing into a checklist. Terrible coping strategy, very efficient though.\n\nSo… no, I can’t point to a relationship I ended where I just didn’t care. I might not always show distress the way people expect, but I don’t think it was absent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever deliberately deceived or lied to someone for personal gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, “personal gain” makes me think of, like, fraud or manipulating someone to get money or a promotion or something, and no, I don’t have anything like that.\n\nHave I ever softened the truth? Sure. Like saying, “I’m almost there,” when I’m absolutely not almost there, which everyone does and somehow we all agree to keep pretending it means something. Or at work, phrasing something more carefully because if you say it too directly people stop hearing the point and start reacting to the wording.\n\nBut deliberately lying to get something for myself… I’m not saying I’ve never told a lie in my life, but I can’t think of an example like that. If anything, I get too caught up in explaining myself, which is probably why a simple question turns into all this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt indifferent to someone else's suffering or well-being?\n\nPatient: Indifferent? I don’t think so, not generally. I mean, there are times where I’m… overwhelmed, or I don’t know what to do with someone else’s pain, so I might get practical instead of warm. Like, “Okay, who do we call, what form needs to be filled out,” which is probably very comforting if what you wanted was a spreadsheet, less so if you wanted a hug.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I don’t care. If anything, I can get stuck thinking about it later, like whether I said the right thing or made it worse. I guess with strangers on the news or something, sometimes you have to shut part of it off because there’s just too much. But that feels different from not caring about an actual person in front of me.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed myself being indifferent to someone’s suffering or well-being. More like awkward or too task-focused sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any significant behavioral problems or get into trouble at school or with the law before you were 15 years old?\n\nPatient: Before 15? I don’t think I had anything major like that. I mean, I got in trouble in the normal school way—talking when I shouldn’t, forgetting homework, probably being sarcastic at exactly the wrong time. Teachers really love that, as you can imagine.\n\nBut significant behavioral problems… I don’t think so. No law trouble or anything like that. I was more of a “please don’t call attention to me” kid, though then again everyone remembers childhood differently, right? Like my version is all cafeteria smells and trying not to wear the wrong shoes on gym day.\n\nSo, no, nothing serious comes to mind before 15.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever bully, threaten, or intimidate others when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, kids can be mean in those small, stupid ways—laughing at someone’s haircut or joining in when a group is making fun of something, and then years later you’re like, wow, children are basically tiny unpaid prosecutors.\n\nBut bullying, threatening, intimidating… no, I don’t remember being that kid. I was more worried about not being targeted myself, honestly, or just trying to blend in.
